<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> Every recursively enumerable set of natural-number tuples has a polynomial Diophantine representation with exactly one complete auxiliary tuple for each member. This proves the single-fold conjecture and, consequently, the finite-fold conjecture.

- **C1** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): For every integer $n\ge1$ and every recursively enumerable set $S\subseteq\mathbb N^n$, there are an integer $m\ge1$ and a polynomial $P\in\mathbb Z[A_1,\ldots,A_n,W_1,\ldots,W_m]$ such that, for every $a\in\mathbb N^n$, $$\#\{w\in\mathbb N^m:P(a,w)=0\}= \begin{cases}1,&a\in S,\\0,&a\notin S.\end{cases}$$
  - Explanation: A polynomial represents each recursively enumerable relation with a unique complete natural witness on members and no witness on nonmembers.
- **C2** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-unique-h10)): There exist integers $m,D\ge1$ for which no algorithm has the following property: on every input polynomial $F\in\mathbb Z[W_1,\ldots,W_m]$ of total degree at most $D$ satisfying the promise $$\#\{w\in\mathbb N^m:F(w)=0\}\le1,$$ the algorithm halts and correctly decides whether this set is nonempty.
  - Explanation: Fixed bounds on degree and witness dimension do not make natural solvability decidable under an at-most-one-solution promise.
- **C3** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-composition)): Finite conjunctions of single-fold polynomial systems, using separate auxiliary tuples for distinct calls, preserve uniqueness for fixed values of all their displayed arguments. Composition of single-fold graphs preserves uniqueness of outputs and complete auxiliary tuples. A finite union of single-fold relations also has a single-fold system if their displayed argument sets are pairwise disjoint.
  - Explanation: Separate internal tuples and zeroed inactive variables preserve uniqueness through conjunction, composition, and disjoint alternatives.
- **C4** (classification, [source](#stmt-prop-rank-one)): The group $E(\mathbb Q)$ has rank one and torsion subgroup $$\mathcal T=\{O,(0,0),(5,0),(-5,0)\}.$$ Consequently there exists $P\in E(\mathbb Q)$ such that every rational point has a unique expression $nP+t$, with $n\in\mathbb Z$ and $t\in\mathcal T$.
  - Explanation: The rational point group splits into an infinite cyclic part and the displayed torsion subgroup.
- **C5** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-rational-codes)): Every rational number has exactly one code satisfying <a href="#eq:rational-code" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rational-code">[eq:rational-code]</a>. Any fixed finite sequence of rational additions, subtractions, multiplications, divisions by nonzero divisors, and comparisons on valid codes has a polynomial implementation with unique outputs and auxiliary coordinates.
  - Explanation: Reduced coordinates and a restricted modular inverse determine a unique complete rational code.
- **C6** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-point-arithmetic)): Given valid codes for $Q_1,Q_2\in E(\mathbb Q)$, addition has a polynomial implementation with a unique output code for $Q_1+Q_2$ and a unique complete tuple of auxiliary coordinates. The same is true of any fixed finite chain of additions and of multiplication by any fixed positive integer.
  - Explanation: Disjoint chord and tangent cases implement the group law with unique complete witnesses.
- **C7** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-representatives)): For each integer $j\ge1$, exactly one point in $(jP+\mathcal T)\cup(-jP+\mathcal T)$ satisfies <a href="#eq:representative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:representative">[eq:representative]</a>. These are all the representatives.
  - Explanation: Maximal x-coordinate and nonnegative y-coordinate select a unique point for each positive absolute index.
- **C8** (construction, [source](#stmt-prop-growth)): There are a total function $g:\mathbb N\to\mathbb N$, a real constant $\delta>0$, and an integer $u_0\ge4$ such that the graph of $g$ has a single-fold Diophantine representation, including uniqueness of its output and every auxiliary coordinate for each input, and $$\label{eq:growth-bounds} \delta(\log u)^2<\log g(u)<u\log u \qquad(u\ge u_0).$$
  - Explanation: Height bracketing and a multiplicative window define a total single-fold graph with intermediate growth.
- **C9** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-pell-sequences)): For an integer $a\ge2$, put $D=a^2-1$ and define integers $x_i(a),y_i(a)$ by $$(a+\sqrt D)^i=x_i(a)+y_i(a)\sqrt D\qquad(i\ge0).$$ Every solution in positive integers of $x^2-Dy^2=1$ is $(x_i(a),y_i(a))$ for exactly one $i\ge1$. Moreover, $$\label{eq:pell-bounds} y_i(a)\equiv i\pmod{a-1},\qquad (2a-1)^{i-1}\le y_i(a)\le(2a)^{i-1}\quad(i\ge1).$$ If $b,u\ge2$ and $n\ge0$ are integers, then $$\label{eq:pell-ratio} b^n\le \frac{y_{n+1}(bu)}{y_{n+1}(u)} \le b^n\left(1+\frac1{2u-1}\right)^n.$$
  - Explanation: Pell sequences enumerate positive solutions and provide congruence, growth, and ratio bounds.
- **C10** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-power)): Suppose that $g:\mathbb N\to\mathbb N$ has a complete single-fold graph and that there are fixed constants $\delta>0$ and $u_0\in\mathbb N$, with $u_0\ge4$, such that $$\label{eq:power-growth-hypothesis} \delta(\log u)^2<\log g(u)<u\log u \qquad(u\ge u_0).$$ Then the relation $$\operatorname{Pow}(b,n,c)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad b\ge2\ \hbox{ and }\ c=b^n \qquad(b,n,c\in\mathbb N)$$ has a complete single-fold representation. In particular, for each $b\ge2$ and $n\in\mathbb N$, the output $c$ and all auxiliary variables are jointly unique.
  - Explanation: The assumed growth graph pins paired Pell indices and recovers powers through a unique quotient.
- **C11** (construction, [source](#stmt-prop-subset)): The relation $x\preccurlyeq y$ on $\mathbb N^2$ has a complete single-fold Diophantine representation.
  - Explanation: Binomial digit extraction and oddness represent binary containment with a complete unique witness.
- **C12** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-counter-universality)): Let $n\geq 1$ and let $S\subseteq\mathbb N^n$ be recursively enumerable. There is a counter machine that, when its first $n$ counters are initialized to $a_1,\ldots,a_n$ and its remaining counters to zero, reaches its halt state if and only if $\mathbf a\in S$.
  - Explanation: A deterministic counter machine accepts any recursively enumerable tuple relation from direct counter inputs.
- **C13** (construction, [source](#stmt-prop-machine-compiler)): Fix $n\ge1$ and a deterministic counter machine with finitely many states and counters, instructions $\operatorname{INC}$ and $\operatorname{TESTDEC}$, and one halt state. Initialize each counter by a fixed choice of an input coordinate $a_i$ or zero. There is a finite system of integer polynomial equations in $a\in\mathbb N^n$ and natural auxiliary variables such that, for every input $a$, the complete auxiliary tuple has exactly one solution when the initialized machine halts, and no solution when it does not halt.
  - Explanation: The run, clock, and block base fix a complete auxiliary tuple exactly when the initialized machine halts.

Composition of single-fold graphs preserves uniqueness of outputs and complete auxiliary tuples. A finite union of single-fold relations also has a single-fold system if their displayed argument sets are pairwise disjoint.",
          "source_id": "PM-242.1"
        }
      ],

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-rank-one",
        "exact_quote": "The group $E(\\mathbb Q)$ has rank one and torsion subgroup $$\\mathcal T=\\{O,(0,0),(5,0),(-5,0)\\}.$$ Consequently there exists $P\\in E(\\mathbb Q)$ such that every rational point has a unique expression $nP+t$, with $n\\in\\mathbb Z$ and $t\\in\\mathcal T$.",
        "source_id": "PM-242.1"
      },

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-pell-sequences",
        "exact_quote": "For an integer $a\\ge2$, put $D=a^2-1$ and define integers $x_i(a),y_i(a)$ by $$(a+\\sqrt D)^i=x_i(a)+y_i(a)\\sqrt D\\qquad(i\\ge0).$$ Every solution in positive integers of $x^2-Dy^2=1$ is $(x_i(a),y_i(a))$ for exactly one $i\\ge1$. Moreover, $$\\label{eq:pell-bounds}\n y_i(a)\\equiv i\\pmod{a-1},\\qquad\n (2a-1)^{i-1}\\le y_i(a)\\le(2a)^{i-1}\\quad(i\\ge1).$$ If $b,u\\ge2$ and $n\\ge0$ are integers, then $$\\label{eq:pell-ratio}\n b^n\\le \\frac{y_{n+1}(bu)}{y_{n+1}(u)}\n \\le b^n\\left(1+\\frac1{2u-1}\\right)^n.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-242.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-pell-sequences",
          "exact_quote": "For an integer $a\\ge2$, put $D=a^2-1$ and define integers $x_i(a),y_i(a)$ by $$(a+\\sqrt D)^i=x_i(a)+y_i(a)\\sqrt D\\qquad(i\\ge0).$$ Every solution in positive integers of $x^2-Dy^2=1$ is $(x_i(a),y_i(a))$ for exactly one $i\\ge1$. Moreover, $$\\label{eq:pell-bounds}\n y_i(a)\\equiv i\\pmod{a-1},\\qquad\n (2a-1)^{i-1}\\le y_i(a)\\le(2a)^{i-1}\\quad(i\\ge1).$$ If $b,u\\ge2$ and $n\\ge0$ are integers, then $$\\label{eq:pell-ratio}\n b^n\\le \\frac{y_{n+1}(bu)}{y_{n+1}(u)}\n \\le b^n\\left(1+\\frac1{2u-1}\\right)^n.$$",
          "source_id": "PM-242.1"
        }
      ],

        "anchor": "stmt-prop-power",
        "exact_quote": "Suppose that $g:\\mathbb N\\to\\mathbb N$ has a complete single-fold graph and that there are fixed constants $\\delta>0$ and $u_0\\in\\mathbb N$, with $u_0\\ge4$, such that $$\\label{eq:power-growth-hypothesis}\n \\delta(\\log u)^2<\\log g(u)<u\\log u\n \\qquad(u\\ge u_0).$$ Then the relation $$\\operatorname{Pow}(b,n,c)\\quad\\Longleftrightarrow\\quad\n b\\ge2\\ \\hbox{ and }\\ c=b^n\n \\qquad(b,n,c\\in\\mathbb N)$$ has a complete single-fold representation.     {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-machine-compiler",
        "exact_quote": "Fix $n\\ge1$ and a deterministic counter machine with finitely many states and counters, instructions $\\operatorname{INC}$ and $\\operatorname{TESTDEC}$, and one halt state. <a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Natural numbers** ([source](#pm-h-2)): Throughout, $\mathbb N=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}$.
- **Recursively enumerable set** ([source](#pm-h-2)): A set $S\subseteq\mathbb N^n$ is *recursively enumerable* if a deterministic computation accepts exactly its members; it may run forever on other inputs.
- **Diophantine representation** ([source](#pm-h-2)): A *Diophantine representation* of $S$ is a polynomial $$P\in\mathbb Z[A_1,\ldots,A_n,W_1,\ldots,W_m]$$ such that $a\in S$ if and only if $P(a,w)=0$ for some $w\in\mathbb N^m$.
- **Single-fold representation** ([source](#pm-h-2)): The representation is *single-fold* if this complete tuple $w$ is unique whenever it exists.
- **Finite-fold representation** ([source](#pm-h-2)): It is *finite-fold* if the set of such tuples is finite for every input.
- **Single-fold polynomial system** ([source](#stmt-auto-definition-1)): A *single-fold polynomial system* for a relation $R\subseteq\mathbb N^d$ is a finite list of integer polynomial equations $F_i(a,z)=0$ such that the set of complete tuples $z$ is a singleton for $a\in R$ and empty otherwise.
- **Rational code** ([source](#eq:rational-code)): A rational number is represented by six natural numbers $(p,n,d,t,s,q)$ satisfying $$\label{eq:rational-code} pn=0,\qquad d=1+t+s,\qquad (p+n)t+d=1+qd.$$
- **Rational height** ([source](#pm-h-9)): For a reduced rational number $a/d$ with $d>0$, define $$H(a/d)=\max\{\|a\|,d\}.$$
- **Binary containment** ([source](#sec:subset)): For $x,y\in\mathbb N$, write $x\preccurlyeq y$ if every binary digit equal to one in $x$ is also equal to one in $y$.

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **closure** [claim](#stmt-lem-composition) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-composition): Gate disjoint cases and force inactive local tuples to zero. -> Complete-witness-preserving composition
- **curve** [claim](#stmt-prop-rank-one) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-machine-compiler-4): Use descent and torsion constraints to identify the rational point group. -> Rank-one decomposition
- **rational** [claim](#stmt-lem-rational-codes) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-rational-codes): Restrict the inverse residue and clear positive denominators. -> Unique rational arithmetic codes
- **points** [claim](#stmt-lem-point-arithmetic) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-point-arithmetic): Use disjoint chord and tangent formulas. -> Unique point arithmetic
- **representatives** [claim](#stmt-lem-representatives) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-representatives): Choose the maximal x-coordinate in a torsion coset and select its sign. -> Unique representatives
- **growth** [claim](#stmt-prop-growth) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-growth): Bracket the input height and use a multiplicative window to square the index. -> Total single-fold growth graph
- **powers** [claim](#stmt-prop-power) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-power): Pin Pell indices using congruences and cutoff bounds, then recover the integer quotient. -> Single-fold power graph
- **containment** [claim](#stmt-prop-subset) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-subset): Extract a binomial coefficient and enforce oddness. -> Single-fold binary containment
- **machine** [claim](#stmt-prop-machine-compiler) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-machine-compiler): Pack deterministic configurations and use a clock interval to fix the base. -> Unique accepting-run witness
- **polynomial** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-machine-compiler-2): Expand subsidiary systems and sum squares. -> Single representing polynomial
- **promise** [claim](#stmt-cor-unique-h10) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-machine-compiler-3): Specialize a fixed polynomial representing a nonrecursive enumerable set. -> Promise undecidability

        {

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **I1**: Uniqueness counts the complete auxiliary tuple, including subsidiary arithmetic witnesses.
- **I2**: Disjoint case composition fixes every inactive local coordinate to zero.
- **I3**: Carry-free binomial digit extraction and oddness test binary containment; division fixes the displayed witnesses.
- **I4**: The main polynomial representation is existential and does not supply numerical arithmetic constants.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **A1**: Use the uniqueness theorem as a general polynomial solvability decision procedure. -> The at-most-one-solution promise still admits an undecidability result.
- **A2**: Treat finite binary containment tests as certification of the whole single-fold construction. -> The power graphs and all their subsidiary witnesses are additional parts of the theorem.
- **A3**: Generate the full representing polynomial directly from unspecified elliptic constants. -> The construction does not give numerical values or a procedure choosing those constants.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The binary-containment proof gives finite binomial digit extraction with exact quotient and remainder witnesses. The executable slice checks these witnesses and parity; it does not construct the complete single-fold power graph or certify the main theorem.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON list of natural-number pairs [x,y].
# PRECONDITIONS: At most 256 pairs, with each coordinate at most 32.
# OUTPUTS: JSON rows with containment truth and exact digit/division witnesses.
# TIME: A bounded number of exact powers and divisions per pair; bit cost depends on y.
# SPACE: Exact integers of bit length proportional to y squared per pair, plus output.
# ARITHMETIC: FLINT fmpz integers only; no floating point or solver assumptions.
# FAILURE: Invalid or out-of-contract input raises an exception and exits nonzero.
import json, sys
from flint import fmpz
pairs = json.loads(sys.stdin.read())
if not isinstance(pairs, list) or not 1 <= len(pairs) <= 256:
    raise ValueError('pair list')
rows = []
for pair in pairs:
    if not isinstance(pair, list) or len(pair) != 2:
        raise ValueError('pair shape')
    x, y = pair
    if any(type(v) is not int or not 0 <= v <= 32 for v in pair):
        raise ValueError('coordinate domain')
    if x > y:
        rows.append({'holds': False, 'witness': None})
        continue
    B = fmpz(2) ** (y + 1)
    H = B ** x
    N = (B + 1) ** y
    quotient, r = divmod(N, H)
    s, d = divmod(quotient, B)
    holds = bool(int(d) % 2 == 1)
    t = int((d - 1) // 2) if holds else None
    rows.append({'holds': holds, 'witness': [int(v) for v in (B,H,N,s,d,r)] + [t]})
print(json.dumps(rows, sort_keys=True, separators=(',', ':')))
```

## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | Fresh auxiliary tuples and normalization of inactive branches provide a contract for composing symbolic arithmetic components without hidden witness choices. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The simulation described is acceptance-preserving counter-machine simulation; the paper supplies no game mechanics or numerical simulation contract. |
| tools | True | weak | inspirational | inferred | The explicit normalization rules can guide symbolic constraint inspection, while the full arithmetic construction leaves numerical constants unspecified. |
| algorithms | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | Deterministic acceptance histories admit a unique encoding once the clock selects the block base; this is a specification pattern rather than a decision algorithm for arbitrary polynomial solvability. |
| data_structures | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | Reduced rational codes and binary containment provide representation invariants for exact arithmetic and masks; complete normalization is essential to uniqueness. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper specifies integer polynomial systems and deterministic computations without an accelerator architecture or a hardware cost model. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Rational points and uniqueness are used for Diophantine definability; the paper states no cryptographic protocol or security assumption. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The acceptance relation is deterministic and the construction provides no learning objective, training procedure or statistical model. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The polynomial systems encode feasibility and acceptance; no optimization objective or operational solution method is supplied. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Uniqueness follows from deterministic machine histories and local arithmetic normalization; no distributed execution or agreement protocol is specified. |
| other (Diophantine representation and exact arithmetic) | True | strong | inspirational | stated | The paper establishes existence of single-fold Diophantine representations and develops exact arithmetic witness conventions; numerical determination of the full representing polynomial remains unspecified. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-existential-constants** (nonconstructive): The representing polynomial exists, but its coefficients and a numerical procedure for choosing the fixed arithmetic constants are not supplied. Blocked use: ['Treating the full representation as an executable compiler with supplied numerical constants.']
- **limit-natural-witness-domain** (formal_scope): The uniqueness promise concerns natural-number solutions; the corollary does not extend it to integer or rational solutions. Blocked use: ['Transferring the solvability promise to integer or rational witness domains.']
- **limit-complete-auxiliaries** (hypothesis): Witness uniqueness counts every subsidiary arithmetic coordinate, not merely the displayed output or computation record. Blocked use: ['Discarding reducedness witnesses, slacks, selectors or subsidiary-call coordinates when claiming uniqueness.']
- **limit-disjoint-guards** (hypothesis): The finite-union closure requires pairwise disjoint displayed argument sets; unused local coordinates must be fixed in inactive cases. Blocked use: ['Combining overlapping guards without establishing unique case selection.', 'Leaving inactive local variables unconstrained.']
- **limit-nonmembership** (formal_scope): Acceptance is semidecidable, and a computation on an input outside the set may continue forever. Blocked use: ['Deciding nonmembership by waiting for an accepting computation to halt.']
- **limit-at-most-one-promise** (edge_case): The promise allows both absence of a complete solution and a unique complete solution. Blocked use: ['Replacing the at-most-one promise with an exactly-one promise.']
- **limit-existential-degree-dimension** (nonconstructive): The fixed degree and witness-dimension bounds in the undecidability corollary have no supplied numerical values. Blocked use: ['Claiming a numerical degree or witness-dimension bound for the promise problem.']
- **limit-encoding-size** (formal_scope): Fixing the degree and witness dimension does not fix the coefficient encoding size under specialization. Blocked use: ['Inferring bounded input bit lengths from fixed degree and witness dimension.']
- **limit-growth-regime** (hypothesis): The power construction requires a complete single-fold growth graph and the stated strict growth inequalities in their threshold regime. Blocked use: ['Substituting a merely observed growth trend or a graph with nonunique internal witnesses into the power construction.']
- **limit-fixed-tuple-not-runtime** (formal_scope): The auxiliary tuple has fixed finite length, while this length does not bound the encoded accepting computation time. Blocked use: ['Inferring a runtime or arithmetic bit-size bound from the fixed number of witness coordinates.']
- **limit-finite-subroutine-scope** (formal_scope): Finite checks of binary digit extraction establish only the checked arithmetic instances; they do not establish the full existential Diophantine representation. Blocked use: ['Using finite arithmetic checks as a certificate of the complete representing polynomial or its unspecified constants.']

>
> ## Scope
>
> The single-fold Diophantine conjecture asks whether every recursively enumerable set has a polynomial representation with a unique auxiliary witness for each member. The formalization establishes this for every recursively enumerable subset of $\mathbb N^n$, $n\ge1$: an integer polynomial has exactly one complete natural-number witness tuple for members and none for nonmembers.
>
> The consequence that solvability remains undecidable under an at-most-one-solution promise is not separately formalized.

Comparator source `SingleFold.lean` (1073 bytes, sha256 `a32b461c1dadec053dce0a3ddc101273021af51e987e949cac44dc5cd4012a98`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

namespace SingleFold

/-- Evaluation over the integers of an integer polynomial at natural inputs. -/
noncomputable def evalNat {n m : ℕ} (P : MvPolynomial (Fin n ⊕ Fin m) ℤ)
    (a : Fin n → ℕ) (w : Fin m → ℕ) : ℤ :=
  MvPolynomial.eval (Sum.elim (fun i => (a i : ℤ)) (fun j => (w j : ℤ))) P

/-- Exact existence on members, no witness on nonmembers, and uniqueness of the
whole witness on every input. No coordinates are hidden outside `w`. -/
def Represents {n m : ℕ} (P : MvPolynomial (Fin n ⊕ Fin m) ℤ)
    (S : Set (Fin n → ℕ)) : Prop :=
  ∀ a, (a ∈ S ↔ ∃ w, evalNat P a w = 0) ∧
    ∀ w v, evalNat P a w = 0 → evalNat P a v = 0 → w = v

/-- Single-fold representation with positive input and witness lengths. -/
def MainStatement : Prop :=
  ∀ (n : ℕ), 1 ≤ n → ∀ (S : Set (Fin n → ℕ)), REPred (fun a => a ∈ S) →
    ∃ (m : ℕ), 1 ≤ m ∧ ∃ P : MvPolynomial (Fin n ⊕ Fin m) ℤ, Represents P S

theorem main : MainStatement := by
  sorry

end SingleFold

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


Every recursively enumerable set of natural-number tuples has a polynomial Diophantine representation with exactly one complete auxiliary tuple for each member. This proves the single-fold conjecture and, consequently, the finite-fold conjecture.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>

<a id="pm-h-2"></a>
### Introduction

Throughout, $\mathbb N=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}$. A set $S\subseteq\mathbb N^n$ is *recursively enumerable* if a deterministic computation accepts exactly its members; it may run forever on other inputs. A *Diophantine representation* of $S$ is a polynomial $$P\in\mathbb Z[A_1,\ldots,A_n,W_1,\ldots,W_m]$$ such that $a\in S$ if and only if $P(a,w)=0$ for some $w\in\mathbb N^m$. The representation is *single-fold* if this complete tuple $w$ is unique whenever it exists. It is *finite-fold* if the set of such tuples is finite for every input. These definitions count all auxiliary variables, including those used to express subsidiary arithmetic operations.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *For every integer $n\ge1$ and every recursively enumerable set $S\subseteq\mathbb N^n$, there are an integer $m\ge1$ and a polynomial $P\in\mathbb Z[A_1,\ldots,A_n,W_1,\ldots,W_m]$ such that, for every $a\in\mathbb N^n$, $$\#\{w\in\mathbb N^m:P(a,w)=0\}=
 \begin{cases}1,&a\in S,\\0,&a\notin S.\end{cases}$$*

</div>

The Davis–Putnam–Robinson theorem established exponential Diophantine representations of recursively enumerable relations: the defining expressions may use exponentiation as well as addition and multiplication (Davis, Putnam, and Robinson 1961, sec. 1) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[dpr1961](#ref-dpr1961)]. Robinson had shown how suitable growth conditions yield an existential definition of exponentiation (Robinson 1952, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[robinson1952](#ref-robinson1952)]. Matiyasevich’s 1970 construction of an exponentially growing Diophantine relation completed this route to polynomial representations (Yu. V. Matiyasevich 1970, sec. 1 and 3) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[mati1970](#ref-mati1970)]. The resulting representation theorem implies the negative solution of Hilbert’s tenth problem, since a decision procedure for polynomial solvability would decide membership in every recursively enumerable set. It does not bound the number of witnesses.

Matiyasevich obtained a different strengthening in 1974: every recursively enumerable relation has an exponential representation with a unique complete auxiliary tuple (Yu. V. Matiyasevich 1974, sec. 2, Main Theorem) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[mati1974](#ref-mati1974)]. The same paper already formulates the remaining problem of polynomial representations with this uniqueness and reduces it to single-fold binary exponentiation (Yu. V. Matiyasevich 1974, sec. 1) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[mati1974](#ref-mati1974)]. This is the single-fold conjecture; allowing finitely many witnesses gives the finite-fold conjecture (Yu. Matiyasevich 2010) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[mati2010](#ref-mati2010)]. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> proves the single-fold assertion over $\mathbb N$. The witness domain matters: Miller and Shlapentokh obtain single-fold definitions of computably enumerable sets of rational integers using witnesses in characteristic-zero polynomial rings, and finite-fold definitions of computably enumerable relations in polynomial rings over rings of integers of totally real number fields (Miller and Shlapentokh 2022) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[millershlapentokh2022](#ref-millershlapentokh2022)].

Our arithmetic construction develops the classical strategy of recovering powers from Pell solutions and controlling their indices through growth bounds. Cantone, Cuzziol, and Omodeo give a recent account, including a paired-Pell representation and the problem of replacing its exponential cutoff (Cantone, Cuzziol, and Omodeo 2024, sec. 6, Lemma 6.1 and Theorem 5; Section 7) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[cco2024](#ref-cco2024)]. We supply a cutoff using rational points on a rank-one elliptic curve. Poonen uses rank-one curves and denominator growth to obtain Diophantine definitions between rings of integers (Poonen 2002, Theorem 1 and Section 2.3, Lemma 8) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[poonen2002](#ref-poonen2002)]. Here the Néron–Tate height compares the full logarithmic x-coordinate height with a quadratic function of an integer index, with uniformly bounded additive error (Silverman 2009, vol. 106, chap. VIII, Proposition 9.1 and Theorem 9.3) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[silverman](#ref-silverman)]. The bounded additive error makes the exact index selection below possible.

The computational construction uses the bit-masking method of Jones and Matiyasevich (Jones and Matiyasevich 1984, 1991) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[jm1984](#ref-jm1984), [jm1991](#ref-jm1991)]. Large-base encodings turn a finite register-machine history into finitely many integers, and Lucas’s binomial congruence tests the permitted binary digits. Determining the base from the input and computation time already appears in Chaitin’s account (Chaitin 1987, vol. 1, sec. 2.3 and 2.6) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[chaitin1987](#ref-chaitin1987)]. We use a clock and a dyadic interval to select one base for the entire run. Throughout both constructions, uniqueness must include reduced rational coordinates, arithmetic quotients and slacks, and the variables of inactive cases. The proof keeps this complete-witness requirement through every substitution.

The arithmetic constants in the proof are fixed once for all sets $S$ and all inputs. They include rational points and integers whose existence is established below, but whose numerical values are not specified. The theorem therefore gives existence of an integer polynomial without displaying its coefficients or a procedure for numerically choosing those constants.

<a id="stmt-cor-unique-h10"></a>
<div id="cor:unique-h10" class="corollary">

**Corollary 2** (Undecidability with an at-most-one-solution promise). *There exist integers $m,D\ge1$ for which no algorithm has the following property: on every input polynomial $F\in\mathbb Z[W_1,\ldots,W_m]$ of total degree at most $D$ satisfying the promise $$\#\{w\in\mathbb N^m:F(w)=0\}\le1,$$ the algorithm halts and correctly decides whether this set is nonempty.*

</div>

The proof appears in Section <a href="#sec:consequences" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:consequences">8</a>, after the construction of the single-fold representation.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Proof overview

The problem is to retain uniqueness when a computation is expressed by polynomial equations. We first obtain unique arithmetic witnesses, then a unique encoding of the accepting run. Section <a href="#sec:conventions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:conventions">2</a> explains how composition and disjoint cases preserve the complete witness, including variables introduced inside subsidiary calls.

The arithmetic step begins with the rational points of $E:y^2=x^3-25x$. Its group is $\mathbb ZP\oplus\mathcal T$, with $\mathcal T$ finite. Section <a href="#sec:elliptic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:elliptic">3</a> gives unique natural-number codes for rational coordinates and point addition; Appendix <a href="#app:rank-one" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:rank-one">9</a> contains the rank calculation. Section <a href="#sec:growth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:growth">4</a> selects one point for each positive absolute index $j=|n|$ among points $nP+t$, where $n\in\mathbb Z$ and $t\in\mathcal T$. Apply a fixed scalar multiplication to each selected point. Let $W_j$ be the maximum of the absolute reduced numerator and positive denominator of its x-coordinate. These integer heights satisfy $$\log W_j=a_Ej^2+O(1),\qquad a_E>0,$$ with a uniformly bounded error. The scalar can enlarge $a_E$ while leaving that error bound fixed; a sufficiently large scalar also makes the sequence strictly increasing.

A discrete difference of the quadratic main term makes exact index selection possible. For positive indices $k,r$, the height product obeys $$\log\frac{W_{2k}W_rW_1}{W_k^2W_{r+1}}
      =2a_E(k^2-r)+O(1).$$ The error is bounded independently of both indices. A fixed multiplicative window for the ratio therefore singles out $r=k^2$, once $a_E$ is large enough to separate distinct integer values of $k^2-r$. The conditions use only products, comparisons and fixed additions of rational points; the indices and logarithms belong to their proof, not to the polynomial system.

For an input $u$, first choose the unique index $k$ with $W_k\le u+W_1<W_{k+1}$, and then select the point at index $k^2$ by this window. The output $g(u)=W_{k^2}$ has a single-fold graph. For large $u$, the quantity $k^2$ is comparable to $\log u$, and the logarithm of the output is comparable to $(\log u)^2$. In particular, for some $\delta>0$ and all sufficiently large $u$, $$\delta(\log u)^2<\log g(u)<u\log u.$$

Section <a href="#sec:power" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:power">5</a> uses these two bounds to select prescribed solutions of two Pell equations. The upper bound forces their indices below a modulus, so congruences determine those indices exactly. The lower bound ensures that the intended solutions are admitted. An integer quotient then recovers $c=b^n$, with a unique complete witness for every $b\ge2$ and $n\ge0$. Section <a href="#sec:subset" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:subset">6</a> uses powers and binomial parity to test whether every nonzero binary digit of one integer is also nonzero in another.

Finally, Section <a href="#sec:computation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:computation">7</a> packs a deterministic counter machine’s states and counter values into digits of finitely many integers. Binary containment restricts the permitted digits, and polynomial identities enforce transitions and the first accepting halt. A clock fixes one power-of-two base for the entire run. Thus every solution decodes to the actual accepting computation, and that computation determines exactly one complete tuple. Substituting the single-fold arithmetic systems and taking a sum of squares proves Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. Appendix <a href="#app:witnesses" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:witnesses">10</a> specifies all coordinates counted in this uniqueness assertion.

<a id="sec:conventions"></a>
### Polynomial constructions with unique witnesses

This section fixes the meaning of the polynomial notation used in the proof. Its purpose is to make uniqueness survive every substitution, case distinction, and auxiliary arithmetic operation.

<a id="stmt-auto-definition-1"></a>
<div class="definition">

**Definition 3**. A *single-fold polynomial system* for a relation $R\subseteq\mathbb N^d$ is a finite list of integer polynomial equations $F_i(a,z)=0$ such that the set of complete tuples $z$ is a singleton for $a\in R$ and empty otherwise. A *single-fold graph* of a function $f:D\to\mathbb N^e$, where $D\subseteq\mathbb N^d$, is such a system for $\{(a,f(a)):a\in D\}$. Thus each input $a\in D$ determines both the output and all further auxiliary variables uniquely. Inputs outside $D$ have no solutions.

</div>

All unspecified variables in polynomial systems range over $\mathbb N$. Integer polynomials may have negative coefficients. In particular, a displayed inequality $F\ge G$ means $F-G=s$ with a new variable $s\in\mathbb N$, and $F>G$ means $F-G=1+s$. The slack is unique; the values of $F$ and $G$ need not separately be nonnegative. An integer $z$ is represented by its unique sign code $z=z^+-z^-$, where $z^+,z^-\in\mathbb N$ and $z^+z^-=0$. Its absolute value is $z^++z^-$. Whenever a polynomial expression is passed as a natural-number argument to a system, it can be replaced by a fresh variable equal to that expression. This introduces a unique variable and enforces the required nonnegativity.

The following closure operations belong to the single-fold constructions of (Yu. V. Matiyasevich 1974, secs. 3.1–3.3) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[mati1974](#ref-mati1974)]. In particular, disjoint alternatives require fixing variables that are unused in the chosen case; see also (Cantone, Cuzziol, and Omodeo 2024, sec. 2, pp. 590–591) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[cco2024](#ref-cco2024)]. We state the precise version needed here.

<a id="stmt-lem-composition"></a>
<div id="lem:composition" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Composition and disjoint cases). *Finite conjunctions of single-fold polynomial systems, using separate auxiliary tuples for distinct calls, preserve uniqueness for fixed values of all their displayed arguments. Composition of single-fold graphs preserves uniqueness of outputs and complete auxiliary tuples. A finite union of single-fold relations also has a single-fold system if their displayed argument sets are pairwise disjoint.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-composition"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-composition</summary>

*Proof.* For conjunction, each auxiliary tuple is fixed by its call’s arguments. In a composition of functions, fix the first input and successively determine every intermediate output and its auxiliary tuple. This proves both existence and uniqueness along the chain.

For a disjoint union with $r$ cases, introduce selectors $e_1,\ldots,e_r$ with $$e_i(e_i-1)=0,\qquad \sum_{i=1}^r e_i=1.$$ Write every equation in case $i$, including its guard and all equations inside nested calls, in the form $F=0$, and replace it by $e_iF=0$. For each variable $z$ local to this case, also impose $(1-e_i)z=0$. This last rule applies to all local coordinates, outputs of local calls, normalization variables, selectors, and slacks, including those inside nested calls. Displayed arguments shared between cases are not local variables. Exactly one selector equals one. Its case has its unique witness tuple, while every other case has all local variables zero. Disjointness makes the selector itself unique. ◻

</details>

When defining a function by cases, we additionally require the guards to partition its input domain and each active case to determine the shared output. This is the form used below.

For example, $h=\max(a,b)$ is obtained from the disjoint cases $a\ge b$, $h=a$ and $a<b$, $h=b$. The strict inequality in the second guard assigns ties to one case. Quotient and remainder on division by a positive integer $d$ are specified by $a=dq+r$ and $r<d$; both and the slack are unique. No unbounded choice of a Bézout coefficient or of an inactive-case variable is permitted in the constructions below.

Point coordinates will be given fixed tuples of natural numbers in Section <a href="#sec:elliptic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:elliptic">3</a>. A single-fold system on such points always means a system on these entire tuples. To use an unprescribed point existentially, its entire code is part of the witness. We will prove uniqueness of that point before appealing to uniqueness of its code.

Finally, a finite system $F_1=\cdots=F_r=0$ is equivalent over $\mathbb N$ to <a id="eq:squares"></a>$$\label{eq:squares}
 F_1^2+\cdots+F_r^2=0.$$ This changes neither its variables nor its solutions. We may consequently work with finite systems until the final conversion to one polynomial.

<a id="sec:elliptic"></a>
### A rank-one curve with unique arithmetic codes

This section supplies unique polynomial codes for the rational points of a rank-one elliptic curve. Its group structure provides the integer index used by the height construction in Section <a href="#sec:growth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:growth">4</a>. We first record that structure, then implement rational coordinates and the group law using the single-fold conventions of Section <a href="#sec:conventions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:conventions">2</a>.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### The curve and its rational points

Let $$E:\quad y^2=x^3-25x$$ denote the nonsingular projective cubic obtained by adjoining $O=[0:1:0]$ to this affine equation. We use its chord–tangent group law with identity $O$. Negation sends $(x,y)$ to $(x,-y)$.

<a id="stmt-prop-rank-one"></a>
<div id="prop:rank-one" class="proposition">

**Proposition 5**. *The group $E(\mathbb Q)$ has rank one and torsion subgroup $$\mathcal T=\{O,(0,0),(5,0),(-5,0)\}.$$ Consequently there exists $P\in E(\mathbb Q)$ such that every rational point has a unique expression $nP+t$, with $n\in\mathbb Z$ and $t\in\mathcal T$.*

</div>

A complete two-isogeny descent proof is given in Appendix <a href="#app:rank-one" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:rank-one">9</a>. The construction below uses the group decomposition in the proposition and the explicit torsion points.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Unique codes for rational coordinates

<a id="eq:rational-code"></a>
We must count all coordinates of a rational-point computation, including the witnesses for reducedness. Restricting a Bézout coefficient to one residue interval is a standard way to make coprimality witnesses unique; see (Cantone, Cuzziol, and Omodeo 2024, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[cco2024](#ref-cco2024)]. The following shifted inverse identity also handles denominator one without a separate case. A rational number is represented by six natural numbers $(p,n,d,t,s,q)$ satisfying $$\label{eq:rational-code}
 pn=0,\qquad d=1+t+s,\qquad (p+n)t+d=1+qd.$$ Its value is $(p-n)/d$. The denominator is positive and $0\le t<d$; the last equation asserts that $t$ is an inverse of $p+n$ modulo $d$.

<a id="stmt-lem-rational-codes"></a>
<div id="lem:rational-codes" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6**. *Every rational number has exactly one code satisfying <a href="#eq:rational-code" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rational-code">[eq:rational-code]</a>. Any fixed finite sequence of rational additions, subtractions, multiplications, divisions by nonzero divisors, and comparisons on valid codes has a polynomial implementation with unique outputs and auxiliary coordinates.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-rational-codes"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-rational-codes</summary>

*Proof.* Put $a=p+n$. Equation <a href="#eq:rational-code" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rational-code">[eq:rational-code]</a> gives $\gcd(a,d)=1$, so the represented fraction is reduced. The constraint $pn=0$ uniquely splits its numerator into its positive and negative parts. Conversely a reduced fraction with positive denominator fixes $p,n,d$. If $d>1$, its inverse modulo $d$ has exactly one representative $t$ in $[0,d-1]$; then $$s=d-1-t,\qquad q=\frac{(p+n)t+d-1}{d}$$ are uniquely determined nonnegative integers. If $d=1$, the equations force $t=s=q=0$. In particular the unique code of zero is $(0,0,1,0,0,0)$.

Clear positive denominators in any prescribed rational arithmetic equation. For instance, if the signed numerator and denominator of the $i$-th code are $a_i,d_i$, the equation $z_1+z_2=z_3$ becomes $a_1d_2d_3+a_2d_1d_3=a_3d_1d_2$. The same procedure applies to products and to any fixed rational expression whose denominators are known to be nonzero. A comparison becomes an integer polynomial comparison after multiplication by positive denominators, and has a unique slack as in Section <a href="#sec:conventions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:conventions">2</a>. When the resulting rational value is uniquely specified, its entire code is unique by the first part. ◻

</details>

A point code consists of two valid rational codes and a flag $o\in\{0,1\}$. For $o=1$, force both rational numerators to zero; this gives the unique dummy coordinate pair $(0,0)$ and represents $O$. For $o=0$, impose the affine curve equation. More explicitly, if the coordinate numerators and denominators are $a_x,d_x,a_y,d_y$, this equation is $$a_y^2d_x^3=d_y^2(a_x^3-25a_xd_x^2).$$ The implications are imposed by multiplication by $o$ or $1-o$. Thus every point of $E(\mathbb Q)$ has exactly one point code; the flag distinguishes $O$ from the affine point $(0,0)$.

<a id="stmt-lem-point-arithmetic"></a>
<div id="lem:point-arithmetic" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7**. *Given valid codes for $Q_1,Q_2\in E(\mathbb Q)$, addition has a polynomial implementation with a unique output code for $Q_1+Q_2$ and a unique complete tuple of auxiliary coordinates. The same is true of any fixed finite chain of additions and of multiplication by any fixed positive integer.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-point-arithmetic"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-point-arithmetic</summary>

*Proof.* The output is a shared valid point code. Use the following disjoint cases, with their guards included in the corresponding systems:

1.  $Q_1=O$: return $Q_2$.

2.  $Q_1\ne O$, $Q_2=O$: return $Q_1$.

3.  Both inputs affine and $x_1<x_2$.

4.  Both inputs affine and $x_1>x_2$.

5.  Both inputs affine, $x_1=x_2$, and $y_1+y_2=0$: return $O$.

6.  Both inputs affine, $x_1=x_2$, $y_1=y_2>0$.

7.  Both inputs affine, $x_1=x_2$, $y_1=y_2<0$.

At equal x-coordinates the curve equation gives $y_1^2=y_2^2$. If the points are not negatives, they therefore have equal nonzero y-coordinates. This proves that the list is exhaustive as well as disjoint.

In Cases 3 and 4 introduce a case-local rational slope code and impose $$\lambda(x_2-x_1)=y_2-y_1.$$ In Cases 6 and 7 instead impose $$2y_1\lambda=3x_1^2-25.$$ The coefficient of $\lambda$ is nonzero in each case, so its value and complete code are unique. In all four cases require an affine output and impose $$x_3=\lambda^2-x_1-x_2,\qquad
 y_3=\lambda(x_1-x_3)-y_1.$$ These are the chord–tangent formulas; they give the required point on the curve. Cases 1, 2, and 5 specify their output directly.

Apply the gated-case construction of Section <a href="#sec:conventions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:conventions">2</a>. In particular, the reducedness equations for a local slope code are gated along with its other equations, and every coordinate of an inactive slope code is forced to zero. Only the shared output code remains globally valid. Thus no inactive branch contributes a choice. Lemma <a href="#lem:rational-codes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rational-codes">6</a> gives uniqueness of every active arithmetic witness. Induction along a fixed finite chain proves the final assertions. ◻

</details>

Fixed rational points may now be inserted by their fixed integer codes. We henceforth fix $P$ as in Proposition <a href="#prop:rank-one" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:rank-one">5</a>, as well as the four points of $\mathcal T$. No assertion that the construction numerically computes this choice of $P$ is needed.

<a id="sec:growth"></a>
### A uniquely represented function with intermediate growth

We now use the curve to construct a total single-fold function whose growth is faster than every fixed power and slower than $u^u$. The group decomposition provides an integer index, but the polynomial systems will quantify only uniquely coded rational points. A height comparison will select both a point at a given scale and a second point whose index is the square of the first. Rank-one elliptic curves have been used in Diophantine definability; see Poonen (Poonen 2002) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[poonen2002](#ref-poonen2002)]. Here we use the full rational x-height: its bounded logarithmic error permits an exact comparison of integer indices. The construction below proves the required single-fold graph directly.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Uniform heights and representatives

For a reduced rational number $a/d$ with $d>0$, define $$H(a/d)=\max\{|a|,d\}.$$ Put $h_x(Q)=\log H(x(Q))$ for affine $Q$, and $h_x(O)=0$. We use the canonical height with normalization $$\widehat h(Q)=\frac12\lim_{N\to\infty}
                  4^{-N}h_x([2^N]Q).$$ The Néron–Tate canonical-height theorem gives existence of this limit, $\widehat h([j]Q)=j^2\widehat h(Q)$, and $\widehat h(Q)>0$ for nontorsion rational $Q$. Since $x$ is an even function of degree two, it also gives a constant $c_E>0$, depending only on this curve and coordinate, such that <a id="eq:uniform-height"></a>$$\label{eq:uniform-height}
 \bigl|\log H(x(Q))-2\widehat h(Q)\bigr|\le c_E
 \qquad(Q\in E(\mathbb Q),\ Q\ne O).$$ These are precisely the uniform comparison and quadraticity statements of (Silverman 2009, vol. 106, chap. VIII, Proposition 9.1 and Theorem 9.3) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[silverman](#ref-silverman)].

<a id="eq:height-constants"></a>
We will compare a bounded height error with the gap between distinct integer indices. A fixed scalar multiplication enlarges the quadratic coefficient while leaving the uniform error bound unchanged. Choose an integer $\kappa>e^{6c_E}$, then choose a positive even integer $m_*$ so large that $a_E=2m_*^2\widehat h(P)$ satisfies $$\label{eq:height-constants}
 a_E>2c_E,
 \qquad 2a_E-6c_E>\log\kappa.$$ Such a choice exists because $\widehat h(P)>0$.

Define a positive integer-valued function on rational points by $$W(Q)=
 \begin{cases}
 H(x([m_*]Q)),&[m_*]Q\ne O,\\
 1,&[m_*]Q=O.
 \end{cases}$$ It has a unique polynomial computation from the point code: compute $[m_*]Q$ using Lemma <a href="#lem:point-arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:point-arithmetic">7</a>, and take the maximum of the absolute numerator $p+n$ and denominator $d$ in its x-code. The dummy x-code at infinity gives the same value one. The two disjoint comparisons used to compute a maximum have unique slacks and selectors.

<a id="eq:quadratic-sequence"></a>
Because $m_*$ kills $\mathcal T$, the value of $W(nP+t)$ depends only on $j=|n|$. Denote it by $W_j$. Then $$\label{eq:quadratic-sequence}
 W_0=1,\qquad
 \log W_j=a_Ej^2+\varepsilon_j,
 \quad |\varepsilon_j|\le c_E\quad(j\ge1).$$ In particular $W_1>1$. For $j\ge1$ the successive logarithmic difference is at least $a_E(2j+1)-2c_E>0$. Hence $(W_j)_{j\ge0}$ is strictly increasing and unbounded.

<a id="eq:representative"></a>
We next choose one point for each positive absolute index. Call a rational point $Q$ a *representative* if it satisfies $$\label{eq:representative}
 \begin{gathered}
 Q\ne O,\qquad y(Q)\ge0,\qquad W(Q)>1,\\
 Q+t\ne O\quad\hbox{and}\quad x(Q)\ge x(Q+t)
       \qquad(t\in\mathcal T).
 \end{gathered}$$ This is a finite polynomial system on its point code, with unique auxiliaries for each point satisfying it: there are only four translates, and their addition and comparison computations are unique.

<a id="stmt-lem-representatives"></a>
<div id="lem:representatives" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8**. *For each integer $j\ge1$, exactly one point in $(jP+\mathcal T)\cup(-jP+\mathcal T)$ satisfies <a href="#eq:representative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:representative">[eq:representative]</a>. These are all the representatives.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-representatives"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-representatives</summary>

*Proof.* The condition $W(Q)>1$ excludes exactly the torsion points by <a href="#eq:quadratic-sequence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quadratic-sequence">[eq:quadratic-sequence]</a>. Every translate of a nontorsion point by torsion is affine. In a coset $jP+\mathcal T$, $j\ne0$, the x-coordinates are distinct: equality of two x-coordinates on the short Weierstrass curve means that the points are equal or negatives. Equality identifies the torsion translates; the negative possibility would imply that $2jP$ is torsion.

The finite coset therefore has a unique point of maximal x-coordinate. The inequalities in <a href="#eq:representative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:representative">[eq:representative]</a> pick precisely this point. Negation sends it to the unique maximal point in the opposite coset, since negation preserves x and permutes $\mathcal T$. Neither maximal point has y-coordinate zero, because such points are torsion. Exactly one of the two has positive y-coordinate. This proves the claim, including exhaustiveness by Proposition <a href="#prop:rank-one" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:rank-one">5</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Selecting and squaring an index

The next construction specifies its points by finitely many height comparisons. Its proof of uniqueness uses the index in $E(\mathbb Q)/\mathcal T$; that index is not an additional variable of the polynomial system.

<a id="stmt-prop-growth"></a>
<div id="prop:growth" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:growth-bounds"></a>
**Proposition 9**. *There are a total function $g:\mathbb N\to\mathbb N$, a real constant $\delta>0$, and an integer $u_0\ge4$ such that the graph of $g$ has a single-fold Diophantine representation, including uniqueness of its output and every auxiliary coordinate for each input, and $$\label{eq:growth-bounds}
 \delta(\log u)^2<\log g(u)<u\log u
       \qquad(u\ge u_0).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-growth"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-growth</summary>

*Proof.* *Selecting an index at the input scale.* The integer $W_1=W(P)$ is now fixed. On input $u\in\mathbb N$, require a representative $Q$ satisfying <a id="eq:index-bracket"></a>$$\label{eq:index-bracket}
 W(Q)\le u+W_1<
          \max\{W(Q+P),W(Q-P)\}.$$ If $Q$ has absolute index $k\ge1$, its two neighbors have W-values $W_{k-1}$ and $W_{k+1}$, in some order. Thus their maximum is $W_{k+1}$. Since the sequence is strictly increasing and unbounded, and $u+W_1\ge W_1$, there is exactly one $k\ge1$ such that $$W_k\le u+W_1<W_{k+1}.$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:representatives" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:representatives">8</a> then gives exactly one permissible point $Q$. This reasoning includes $u=0$, for which $k=1$.

<a id="eq:square-window"></a>
*Selecting the squared index.* Require a second representative $R$. To derive the comparisons that will determine it, write $r\ge1$ for its absolute index. Like $k$, this is notation for the proof, not a variable of the system. We want to impose $r=k^2$ using only point operations and height comparisons. The quadratic main terms in <a href="#eq:quadratic-sequence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quadratic-sequence">[eq:quadratic-sequence]</a> suggest the identity $$(2k)^2-2k^2-\bigl((r+1)^2-r^2\bigr)+1=2(k^2-r).$$ Thus the first difference in $r$ cancels its quadratic term, while doubling $k$ leaves a multiple of $k^2$. The same linear combination of logarithmic heights therefore tests $k^2-r=0$ with bounded error. Products express this test without logarithms: using the unique point and height computations, form the positive integers $$\begin{aligned}
 L_0&=W([2]Q)\,W(R)\,W_1,\\
 D_0&=W(Q)^2\max\{W(R+P),W(R-P)\},
\end{aligned}$$ and impose $$\label{eq:square-window}
 L_0\le\kappa D_0,\qquad D_0\le\kappa L_0.$$ The neighbor maximum in $D_0$ equals $W_{r+1}$, by the same argument as for $Q$. All indices in the following calculation are positive, so <a href="#eq:quadratic-sequence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quadratic-sequence">[eq:quadratic-sequence]</a> gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \log\frac{L_0}{D_0}
 &=a_E\bigl((2k)^2+r^2+1-2k^2-(r+1)^2\bigr)+e\\
 &=2a_E(k^2-r)+e,\qquad |e|\le6c_E.
\end{aligned}$$ The error has six terms when multiplicity is counted: one each from $W_{2k},W_r,W_1,W_{r+1}$, and two from $W_k$. If $r=k^2$, its absolute value is less than $\log\kappa$, so <a href="#eq:square-window" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:square-window">[eq:square-window]</a> holds. If $r\ne k^2$, integer separation and <a href="#eq:height-constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-constants">[eq:height-constants]</a> give $$\left|\log\frac{L_0}{D_0}\right|
     \ge2a_E-6c_E>\log\kappa,$$ so the window fails. Therefore exactly the representative of absolute index $k^2$ can serve as $R$. Figure <a href="#fig:height-window" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:height-window">1</a> illustrates this separation.

*Totality and unique witnesses.* Set the output to $$g(u)=W(R)=W_{k^2}.$$ The construction uses only fixed addition chains, rational point codes, maxima, products, and comparisons. It is therefore a finite polynomial system. For every $u$, the bracket pins $Q$ and the window pins $R$. Once these points are fixed, their complete codes, every intermediate point and height, all comparison slacks, and all branch selectors are fixed. Lemmas <a href="#lem:rational-codes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rational-codes">6</a> and <a href="#lem:point-arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:point-arithmetic">7</a>, together with the inactive-variable convention, give uniqueness of every expanded auxiliary. Conversely those uniquely specified points and computations satisfy the system. This proves totality and the single-fold assertion before any asymptotic cutoff is chosen.

<a id="eq:index-growth-estimate"></a>
*Growth estimates.* Write $U=u+W_1$. The bracket and <a href="#eq:quadratic-sequence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quadratic-sequence">[eq:quadratic-sequence]</a> imply $$\label{eq:index-growth-estimate}
 a_Ek^2-c_E\le\log U
   <a_E(k+1)^2+c_E\le4a_Ek^2+c_E.$$ For all sufficiently large $u$, the upper estimate on $\log U$ gives $k^2>\log u/(8a_E)$. Hence $$\log g(u)\ge a_Ek^4-c_E
       >\frac{(\log u)^2}{128a_E}$$ eventually. We may take $\delta=1/(128a_E)$. The other estimate in <a href="#eq:index-growth-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:index-growth-estimate">[eq:index-growth-estimate]</a> gives $$\log g(u)\le a_Ek^4+c_E
    \le\frac{(\log(u+W_1)+c_E)^2}{a_E}+c_E
    =O((\log u)^2).$$ This is eventually strictly less than $u\log u$. Choose an integer $u_0\ge4$ beyond both thresholds, proving <a href="#eq:growth-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:growth-bounds">[eq:growth-bounds]</a>. ◻

</details>

The real constants used in the estimates are not variables or coefficients of the polynomial system. They justify choices of fixed integers, while all fixed rational points enter through integer codes. Proposition <a href="#prop:growth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:growth">9</a> is thus an existence statement about an integer polynomial; no numerical determination of a generator or of the height constants is asserted.

<figure id="fig:height-window">

<figcaption>The index window, schematically for $k ≥ 2$. At index $r$, the logarithmic ratio lies within $6c_{E}$ of $2a_{E}(k^{2}−r)$. The choice $6c_{E} < log κ < 2a_{E} − 6c_{E}$ puts the entire middle interval in the admissible band and every other integer-index interval outside it. The error bars represent bounds, not additional variables.</figcaption>
</figure>
